It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedChemistryCalculationsSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Moles from mass: 25 g of potassium chloride JAH-PSCCS-000001 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: moles from mass: 25 g of potassium chloride. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Converting a weighed mass into moles is the first step of almost every quantitative chemistry calculation: moles, not grams, are what chemical equations count. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The mole was adopted as the SI unit of amount of substance in 1971, and since 2019 it is defined by fixing the Avogadro constant at exactly 6.02214076 x 10^23 per mole. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: moles from mass: 25 g of potassium chloride. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Converting a weighed mass into moles is the first step of almost every quantitative chemistry calculation: moles, not grams, are what chemical equations count. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

How many moles are in 25 g of potassium chloride (KCl)? Use the molar mass of KCl, 74.55 g/mol, and give the answer in moles.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the defining relation: number of moles n equals mass m divided by molar mass M, n = m / M.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: mass m = 25.0 g of potassium chloride (KCl), molar mass M = 74.55 g/mol.
  3. Step 3: Substitute: n = 25.0 / 74.55.
  4. Step 4: Divide: n = 0.3353 mol.
  5. Step 5: Check the units: grams divided by grams per mole leaves moles, as required; check the size: one mole weighs 74.55 g, so 25.0 g is well under one mole, and 0.3353 mol is the right order of magnitude.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

There are 0.3353 mol in the sample. In words: 25.0 g of the compound, whose molar mass is 74.55 g/mol, is 0.3353 mol. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same relation converts any weighed mass of any pure compound into moles, given that compound's molar mass. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for a pure sample of the stated compound. Impurities, a hydrate with waters of crystallisation, or a wrong molar mass all change the answer, and the result says nothing about the sample's volume or concentration. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

The division m / M is exact arithmetic on the stated inputs, and the demo performs it in code. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

The mole counts entities: one mole of a substance contains Avogadro's number of formula units, and the molar mass is the mass of that many units, so dividing mass by molar mass counts the units present. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'mass': 25.0, 'molar_mass': 74.55, 'compound': 'potassium chloride (KCl)'}
def solve(P):
    moles = P['mass'] / P['molar_mass']
    return {'moles': moles, 'mass': P['mass'], 'molar_mass': P['molar_mass']}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Mass (g):', P['mass'])
print('Molar mass (g/mol):', P['molar_mass'])
print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Mass (g): 25.0
Molar mass (g/mol): 74.55
Moles (mol): 0.3353
Run 1 SHA-256: 3ec0342e7702c7d8c45ab22e9aa45a18c4156cc71c690dab34a05970549e006d
Run 2 SHA-256: 3ec0342e7702c7d8c45ab22e9aa45a18c4156cc71c690dab34a05970549e006d
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Moles from mass: 25 g of potassium chloride """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'mass': 25.0, 'molar_mass': 74.55, 'compound': 'potassium chloride (KCl)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        moles = P['mass'] / P['molar_mass']
        return {'moles': moles, 'mass': P['mass'], 'molar_mass': P['molar_mass']}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Mass (g):', P['mass'])
        print('Molar mass (g/mol):', P['molar_mass'])
        print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Molarity: prepare 2 L of 0.25 M potassium chloride JAH-PSCCS-000002 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: molarity: prepare 2 l of 0.25 m potassium chloride. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Preparing a solution of a stated molarity is the routine laboratory calculation behind every standard solution: moles first, then mass. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

Molarity (amount concentration) has been the standard way of stating solution concentration since the nineteenth century, when volumetric analysis made accurate standard solutions essential. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: molarity: prepare 2 l of 0.25 m potassium chloride. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Preparing a solution of a stated molarity is the routine laboratory calculation behind every standard solution: moles first, then mass. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

What mass of potassium chloride (KCl) is needed to prepare 2 L of a 0.25 M KCl solution? The molar mass of KCl is 74.55 g/mol.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the defining relation: molarity C equals moles n divided by volume V in litres, so n = C x V.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: C = 0.25 mol/L, V = 2.0 L of potassium chloride (KCl) solution.
  3. Step 3: Multiply: n = 0.25 x 2.0 = 0.5000 mol.
  4. Step 4: Convert moles to mass with m = n x M: m = 0.5000 x 74.55 = 37.27 g.
  5. Step 5: Check the units: mol/L times litres gives moles; moles times grams per mole gives grams. Weigh 37.27 g, dissolve it, and make the volume up to 2.0 L.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

37.27 g of the compound is needed. That is 0.5000 mol dissolved and made up to 2.0 L, giving a 0.25 M solution. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same two steps prepare any molarity of any soluble compound, given its molar mass and the target volume. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if the solute dissolves completely and the volume is made up to the stated mark after dissolving; adding the solute to the full volume of solvent gives a slightly larger total volume and a slightly lower concentration. Temperature changes the volume slightly. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Two multiplications, n = C x V and m = n x M, each checked by units, and the demo performs both in code. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Molarity counts moles per litre of solution, so the moles needed are concentration times volume, and the mass follows from the molar mass; dissolving and making up to volume gives the stated concentration. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'volume_L': 2.0, 'molarity': 0.25, 'molar_mass': 74.55, 'compound': 'potassium chloride (KCl)'}
def solve(P):
    moles = P['molarity'] * P['volume_L']
    mass = moles * P['molar_mass']
    return {'moles': moles, 'mass': mass}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Volume (L):', P['volume_L'])
print('Molarity (mol/L):', P['molarity'])
print('Moles needed (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
print('Mass needed (g): %.2f' % R['mass'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Volume (L): 2.0
Molarity (mol/L): 0.25
Moles needed (mol): 0.5000

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Molarity: prepare 2 L of 0.25 M potassium chloride """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'volume_L': 2.0, 'molarity': 0.25, 'molar_mass': 74.55, 'compound': 'potassium chloride (KCl)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        moles = P['molarity'] * P['volume_L']
        mass = moles * P['molar_mass']
        return {'moles': moles, 'mass': mass}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Volume (L):', P['volume_L'])
        print('Molarity (mol/L):', P['molarity'])
        print('Moles needed (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
        print('Mass needed (g): %.2f' % R['mass'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

Molarity (amount concentration) has been the standard way of stating solution concentration since the nineteenth century, when volumetric analysis made accurate standard solutions essential. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

What mass of potassium chloride (KCl) is needed to prepare 2 L of a 0.25 M KCl solution? The molar mass of KCl is 74.55 g/mol.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the defining relation: molarity C equals moles n divided by volume V in litres, so n = C x V. Step 2: Identify the inputs: C = 0.25 mol/L, V = 2.0 L of potassium chloride (KCl) solution. Step 3: Multiply: n = 0.25 x 2.0 = 0.5000 mol. Step 4: Convert moles to mass with m = n x M: m = 0.5000 x 74.55 = 37.27 g. Step 5: Check the units: mol/L times litres gives moles; moles times grams per mole gives grams. Weigh 37.27 g, dissolve it, and make the volume up to 2.0 L. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Textbook values: NaCl molar mass 58.44 g/mol, KCl 74.55 g/mol; target volumes and molarities stated in the problem.

Formal equation, in plain words

Scientific equation

C = n / V

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it multiplies molarity by volume to get moles, then multiplies by the molar mass to get the mass. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

37.27 g of the compound is needed. That is 0.5000 mol dissolved and made up to 2.0 L, giving a 0.25 M solution. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only if the solute dissolves completely and the volume is made up to the stated mark after dissolving; adding the solute to the full volume of solvent gives a slightly larger total volume and a slightly lower concentration. Temperature changes the volume slightly. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Dilution is the daily arithmetic of the laboratory: one stock solution, many working concentrations, with the moles conserved at every step. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. The Problem

How much water must be added to 50 mL of 3.0 M stock solution to make a 0.75 M solution? Give the final volume and the volume of water to add.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the dilution relation: moles are conserved, so C1 x V1 = C2 x V2, and V2 = C1 x V1 / C2.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: C1 = 3.0 M, V1 = 50.0 mL, C2 = 0.75 M.
  3. Step 3: Substitute: V2 = 3.0 x 50.0 / 0.75 = 200.0 mL.
  4. Step 4: Water to add is the difference: 200.0 - 50.0 = 150.0 mL.
  5. Step 5: Check: the target is weaker than the stock, so the final volume must exceed the starting volume, and 200.0 mL does; the moles check is C1 x V1 = C2 x V2 on both sides.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The final volume is 200.0 mL, so 150.0 mL of water must be added to the 50.0 mL of stock. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same relation dilutes any solution to any weaker concentration, given the stock concentration and volume. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if volumes add (they do, to a good approximation, for dilute aqueous solutions) and the solute does not react with the solvent. It does not apply if the target is more concentrated than the stock, which would need evaporation or more solute, not dilution. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One division and one subtraction, both checked by the moles balance, and the demo performs both in code. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Diluting adds solvent only, so the moles of solute do not change; equating moles before and after gives C1 x V1 = C2 x V2, and the water added is the volume difference. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'c1': 3.0, 'v1_mL': 50.0, 'c2': 0.75}
def solve(P):
    v2_mL = P['c1'] * P['v1_mL'] / P['c2']
    water_mL = v2_mL - P['v1_mL']
    return {'v2_mL': v2_mL, 'water_mL': water_mL}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Stock concentration (M):', P['c1'])
print('Stock volume (mL):', P['v1_mL'])
print('Target concentration (M):', P['c2'])
print('Final volume (mL): %.1f' % R['v2_mL'])
print('Water to add (mL): %.1f' % R['water_mL'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Dilution: 50 mL of 3.0 M stock to 0.75 M """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'c1': 3.0, 'v1_mL': 50.0, 'c2': 0.75}
    def solve(self, P):

        water_mL = v2_mL - P['v1_mL']

    def run(self):

        R2 = self.solve(P)

        print('Target concentration (M):', P['c2'])

        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Dilution: 50 mL of 3.0 M stock to 0.75 M """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'c1': 3.0, 'v1_mL': 50.0, 'c2': 0.75}
    def solve(self, P):
        v2_mL = P['c1'] * P['v1_mL'] / P['c2']
        water_mL = v2_mL - P['v1_mL']
        return {'v2_mL': v2_mL, 'water_mL': water_mL}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Stock concentration (M):', P['c1'])
        print('Stock volume (mL):', P['v1_mL'])
        print('Target concentration (M):', P['c2'])
        print('Final volume (mL): %.1f' % R['v2_mL'])
        print('Water to add (mL): %.1f' % R['water_mL'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

pH of 0.005 M hydrochloric acid JAH-PSCCS-000004 · simple

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: ph of 0.005 m hydrochloric acid. The pH of a strong acid is the textbook first example of the pH scale: complete dissociation makes the hydrogen-ion concentration equal to the acid concentration. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The pH scale was introduced by S. P. L. Sorensen in 1909 to give a compact logarithmic measure of acidity; the definition pH = -log10[H+] is unchanged since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: ph of 0.005 m hydrochloric acid. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The pH of a strong acid is the textbook first example of the pH scale: complete dissociation makes the hydrogen-ion concentration equal to the acid concentration. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

What is the pH of a 0.005 M solution of hydrochloric acid (HCl), a strong acid that dissociates completely? Give the pH to two decimal places.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the definition: pH = -log10 of the hydrogen-ion concentration, pH = -log10[H+].
  2. Step 2: Identify the input: HCl is a strong acid and dissociates completely, so [H+] equals the acid concentration, 0.005 M.
  3. Step 3: Take the logarithm: log10(0.005) = -2.30.
  4. Step 4: Change the sign: pH = 2.30.
  5. Step 5: Check the sense: a 0.01 M strong acid is strongly acidic, so a pH near 2 is expected, and 2.30 matches.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The pH is 2.30. The solution is strongly acidic, with a hydrogen-ion concentration of 0.005 M. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same definition gives the pH of any strong monoprotic acid solution from its concentration. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for a strong acid at a concentration where complete dissociation is a good approximation and activity corrections are negligible (roughly 0.001 M to 0.1 M at room temperature). Very dilute or very concentrated acids, weak acids, and non-aqueous solvents need different treatment. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One base-10 logarithm with a sign change, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Strong acids dissociate completely in water, so every HCl formula unit yields one hydrogen ion; the pH definition then turns the concentration directly into the scale value. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'conc': 0.005, 'acid': 'hydrochloric acid (HCl)'}
def solve(P):
    import math as _m
    logval = _m.log10(P['conc'])
    ph = -logval
    return {'ph': ph, 'h_conc': P['conc'], 'logval': logval}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Acid concentration (M):', P['conc'])
print('Hydrogen ion concentration (M):', P['conc'])
print('pH: %.2f' % R['ph'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: pH of 0.005 M hydrochloric acid """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'conc': 0.005, 'acid': 'hydrochloric acid (HCl)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        import math as _m
        logval = _m.log10(P['conc'])
        ph = -logval
        return {'ph': ph, 'h_conc': P['conc'], 'logval': logval}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Acid concentration (M):', P['conc'])
        print('Hydrogen ion concentration (M):', P['conc'])
        print('pH: %.2f' % R['ph'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: ph of 0.01 m sodium hydroxide. The pH of a strong base runs through pOH: complete dissociation gives the hydroxide concentration, and the water ion product links pOH to pH. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. The Problem

What is the pH of a 0.01 M solution of sodium hydroxide (NaOH), a strong base that dissociates completely? Give the pH to two decimal places, using Kw = 1.0 x 10^-14 at 25 C.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the relations: pOH = -log10[OH-], and at 25 C, pH + pOH = 14.
  2. Step 2: Identify the input: NaOH is a strong base and dissociates completely, so [OH-] equals the base concentration, 0.01 M.
  3. Step 3: Compute pOH: pOH = -log10(0.01) = 2.00.
  4. Step 4: Subtract from 14: pH = 14 - 2.00 = 12.00.
  5. Step 5: Check the sense: a dilute strong base is distinctly alkaline, so a pH above 7 and near 12.00 is expected.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The pH is 12.00 (pOH 2.00). The solution is alkaline, with a hydroxide-ion concentration of 0.01 M. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same two steps give the pH of any strong base solution from its concentration at 25 C. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only at 25 C, where Kw = 1.0 x 10^-14, and only where complete dissociation and negligible activity corrections are good approximations. At other temperatures pH + pOH is not 14, and weak bases need an equilibrium calculation. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One logarithm and one subtraction, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Strong bases dissociate completely, so [OH-] equals the base concentration; the ion product of water ties [H+] and [OH-] together, so pOH determines pH. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'conc': 0.01, 'base': 'sodium hydroxide (NaOH)'}
def solve(P):
    import math as _m
    poh = -_m.log10(P['conc'])
    ph = 14.0 - poh
    return {'ph': ph, 'poh': poh, 'oh_conc': P['conc']}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Base concentration (M):', P['conc'])
print('Hydroxide ion concentration (M):', P['conc'])
print('pOH: %.2f' % R['poh'])
print('pH: %.2f' % R['ph'])

_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: pH of 0.01 M sodium hydroxide """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'conc': 0.01, 'base': 'sodium hydroxide (NaOH)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        import math as _m
        poh = -_m.log10(P['conc'])
        ph = 14.0 - poh
        return {'ph': ph, 'poh': poh, 'oh_conc': P['conc']}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Base concentration (M):', P['conc'])
        print('Hydroxide ion concentration (M):', P['conc'])
        print('pOH: %.2f' % R['poh'])
        print('pH: %.2f' % R['ph'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

The relation pH + pOH = 14 at 25 C follows from the ion product of water, Kw = 1.0 x 10^-14, measured in the early twentieth century and standard in textbooks ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

What is the pH of a 0.01 M solution of sodium hydroxide (NaOH), a strong base that dissociates completely? Give the pH to two decimal places, using Kw = 1.0 x 10^-14 at 25 C.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the relations: pOH = -log10[OH-], and at 25 C, pH + pOH = 14. Step 2: Identify the input: NaOH is a strong base and dissociates completely, so [OH-] equals the base concentration, 0.01 M. Step 3: Compute pOH: pOH = -log10(0.01) = 2.00. Step 4: Subtract from 14: pH = 14 - 2.00 = 12.00. Step 5: Check the sense: a dilute strong base is distinctly alkaline, so a pH above 7 and near 12.00 is expected. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Base concentrations stated in the problem; Kw = 1.0 x 10^-14 at 25 C.

Formal equation

pOH = -log10[OH-]; pH = 14 - pOH

Formal equation, in plain words

pOH is the logarithmic hydroxide scale, [OH-] is the hydroxide-ion concentration in mol/L, pH is the acidity scale, 14 is -log10 Kw at 25 C.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

Kw = [H+][OH-] = 1.0 x 10^-14

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it takes the negative base-10 logarithm of the stated concentration to get pOH, then subtracts from 14. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

The pH is 12.00 (pOH 2.00). The solution is alkaline, with a hydroxide-ion concentration of 0.01 M. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only at 25 C, where Kw = 1.0 x 10^-14, and only where complete dissociation and negligible activity corrections are good approximations. At other temperatures pH + pOH is not 14, and weak bases need an equilibrium calculation. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Finding the limiting reagent is the central skill of reaction stoichiometry: the equation fixes the reacting ratio, and the reagent that runs out first fixes how much product can form. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: stoichiometry: hydrogen and oxygen, oxygen limiting. Finding the limiting reagent is the central skill of reaction stoichiometry: the equation fixes the reacting ratio, and the reagent that runs out first fixes how much product can form. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

10 g of hydrogen gas (H2) and 40 g of oxygen gas (O2) react to form water: 2 H2 + O2 -> 2 H2O. Which reagent is limiting, what mass of water forms, and what mass of the excess reagent remains? Molar masses: H2 2.016 g/mol, O2 32.00 g/mol, H2O 18.015 g/mol.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Convert each mass to moles: H2, 10.0 g / 2.016 g/mol = 4.9603 mol; O2, 40.0 g / 32.00 g/mol = 1.2500 mol.
  2. Step 2: Read the equation 2 H2 + O2 -> 2 H2O: each mole of O2 needs 2 moles of H2, so 1.2500 mol of O2 needs 2.5000 mol of H2.
  3. Step 3: Compare: 4.9603 mol of H2 is available against 2.5000 mol needed, so the limiting reagent is O2.
  4. Step 4: Water formed follows the limiting reagent: 2.5000 mol of H2O, which is 2.5000 x 18.015 = 45.04 g.
  5. Step 5: Excess remaining: the excess reagent is used only up to the stoichiometric amount, leaving 4.96 g unreacted.
  6. Step 6: Check by mass balance: the mass of water plus the excess equals the starting total mass, within rounding.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The limiting reagent is O2. 45.04 g of water forms (2.5000 mol), and 4.96 g of the excess reagent remains unreacted. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same method finds the limiting reagent and the theoretical yield for any balanced reaction, given the starting masses. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This assumes the reaction goes to completion with no side reactions and pure reagents. In practice yields are lower, competing reactions consume reagents, and impure starting materials change the numbers; the theoretical yield here is an upper bound. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Two divisions, one ratio comparison, and two multiplications, all evaluated in code by the demo, with a mass-balance check at the end. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

A balanced equation states the exact mole ratio in which substances react, so converting masses to moles and comparing with that ratio identifies the reagent that is exhausted first, and the product follows from it. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'h2_g': 10.0, 'o2_g': 40.0}
def solve(P):
    h2_mol = P['h2_g'] / 2.016
    o2_mol = P['o2_g'] / 32.00
    h2_needed = 2.0 * o2_mol
    o2_needed = h2_mol / 2.0
    limiting = 'H2' if h2_needed > h2_mol else 'O2'
    water_mol = 2.0 * o2_mol if limiting == 'O2' else h2_mol
    water_g = water_mol * 18.015
    excess_g = (h2_mol - h2_needed) * 2.016 if limiting == 'O2' else (o2_mol - o2_needed) * 32.00
    return {'h2_mol': h2_mol, 'o2_mol': o2_mol, 'needed': h2_needed, 'water_mol': water_mol, 'water_g': water_g, 'excess_g': excess_g, 'limiting': limiting}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Moles of H2: %.4f' % R['h2_mol'])
print('Moles of O2: %.4f' % R['o2_mol'])
print('Limiting reagent:', R['limiting'])
print('Moles of H2O formed: %.4f' % R['water_mol'])
print('Mass of H2O formed (g): %.2f' % R['water_g'])
print('Excess reagent remaining (g): %.2f' % R['excess_g'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Stoichiometry: hydrogen and oxygen, oxygen limiting """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'h2_g': 10.0, 'o2_g': 40.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        h2_mol = P['h2_g'] / 2.016
        o2_mol = P['o2_g'] / 32.00
        h2_needed = 2.0 * o2_mol
        o2_needed = h2_mol / 2.0
        limiting = 'H2' if h2_needed > h2_mol else 'O2'
        water_mol = 2.0 * o2_mol if limiting == 'O2' else h2_mol
        water_g = water_mol * 18.015
        excess_g = (h2_mol - h2_needed) * 2.016 if limiting == 'O2' else (o2_mol - o2_needed) * 32.00
        return {'h2_mol': h2_mol, 'o2_mol': o2_mol, 'needed': h2_needed, 'water_mol': water_mol, 'water_g': water_g, 'excess_g': excess_g, 'limiting': limiting}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Moles of H2: %.4f' % R['h2_mol'])
        print('Moles of O2: %.4f' % R['o2_mol'])
        print('Limiting reagent:', R['limiting'])
        print('Moles of H2O formed: %.4f' % R['water_mol'])
        print('Mass of H2O formed (g): %.2f' % R['water_g'])
        print('Excess reagent remaining (g): %.2f' % R['excess_g'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'h2_g': 10.0, 'o2_g': 40.0}
def solve(P):
    h2_mol = P['h2_g'] / 2.016
    o2_mol = P['o2_g'] / 32.00
    h2_needed = 2.0 * o2_mol
    o2_needed = h2_mol / 2.0
    limiting = 'H2' if h2_needed > h2_mol else 'O2'
    water_mol = 2.0 * o2_mol if limiting == 'O2' else h2_mol
    water_g = water_mol * 18.015
    excess_g = (h2_mol - h2_needed) * 2.016 if limiting == 'O2' else (o2_mol - o2_needed) * 32.00
    return {'h2_mol': h2_mol, 'o2_mol': o2_mol, 'needed': h2_needed, 'water_mol': water_mol, 'water_g': water_g, 'excess_g': excess_g, 'limiting': limiting}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Moles of H2: %.4f' % R['h2_mol'])
print('Moles of O2: %.4f' % R['o2_mol'])
print('Limiting reagent:', R['limiting'])
print('Moles of H2O formed: %.4f' % R['water_mol'])
print('Mass of H2O formed (g): %.2f' % R['water_g'])
print('Excess reagent remaining (g): %.2f' % R['excess_g'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Stoichiometry: hydrogen and oxygen, oxygen limiting """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'h2_g': 10.0, 'o2_g': 40.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        h2_mol = P['h2_g'] / 2.016
        o2_mol = P['o2_g'] / 32.00
        h2_needed = 2.0 * o2_mol
        o2_needed = h2_mol / 2.0
        limiting = 'H2' if h2_needed > h2_mol else 'O2'
        water_mol = 2.0 * o2_mol if limiting == 'O2' else h2_mol
        water_g = water_mol * 18.015
        excess_g = (h2_mol - h2_needed) * 2.016 if limiting == 'O2' else (o2_mol - o2_needed) * 32.00
        return {'h2_mol': h2_mol, 'o2_mol': o2_mol, 'needed': h2_needed, 'water_mol': water_mol, 'water_g': water_g, 'excess_g': excess_g, 'limiting': limiting}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Moles of H2: %.4f' % R['h2_mol'])
        print('Moles of O2: %.4f' % R['o2_mol'])
        print('Limiting reagent:', R['limiting'])
        print('Moles of H2O formed: %.4f' % R['water_mol'])
        print('Mass of H2O formed (g): %.2f' % R['water_g'])
        print('Excess reagent remaining (g): %.2f' % R['excess_g'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: percent yield: 60 g obtained from 80 g theoretical. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Percent yield is the single number that summarises how completely a real reaction delivered what the stoichiometry promised. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

A reaction has a theoretical yield of 80.0 g of product, and 60.0 g is actually obtained. What is the percent yield?

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the definition: percent yield = (actual yield / theoretical yield) x 100%.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: actual = 60.0 g, theoretical = 80.0 g.
  3. Step 3: Divide: 60.0 / 80.0 = 0.750.
  4. Step 4: Multiply by 100: 75.0%.
  5. Step 5: Check the sense: the actual yield is below the theoretical yield, so a value below 100% is expected, and 75.0% is.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The percent yield is 75.0%. Of the 80.0 g that could have formed in theory, 60.0 g was actually obtained. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same ratio reports the yield of any reaction, given the theoretical and actual masses of the same product. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if the theoretical yield is computed correctly from the limiting reagent and the actual yield is the mass of pure product. An impure product inflates the apparent yield, and a percent yield above 100% always signals an error or an impurity, never a better reaction. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One division and one multiplication by 100, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

The theoretical yield is the maximum the limiting reagent allows; the actual yield is what is isolated, and their ratio measures the combined effect of incomplete reaction, side reactions, and losses in handling. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'theoretical_g': 80.0, 'actual_g': 60.0}
def solve(P):
    ratio = P['actual_g'] / P['theoretical_g']
    pct = 100.0 * ratio
    return {'pct': pct, 'ratio': ratio}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Theoretical yield (g):', P['theoretical_g'])
print('Actual yield (g):', P['actual_g'])
print('Percent yield (%%): %.1f' % R['pct'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Percent yield: 60 g obtained from 80 g theoretical """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'theoretical_g': 80.0, 'actual_g': 60.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        ratio = P['actual_g'] / P['theoretical_g']
        pct = 100.0 * ratio
        return {'pct': pct, 'ratio': ratio}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Theoretical yield (g):', P['theoretical_g'])
        print('Actual yield (g):', P['actual_g'])
        print('Percent yield (%%): %.1f' % R['pct'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Gas volume at STP: 3 mol of gas JAH-PSCCS-000008 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: gas volume at stp: 3 mol of gas. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The molar volume at STP is the quickest route from moles of gas to litres: one number, one multiplication, and the volume follows. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

Avogadro's hypothesis (1811) that equal volumes of gases hold equal numbers of molecules led to the molar volume; the modern STP value of 22.414 L/mol follows from the ideal gas law at 0 C and 1 atm. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: gas volume at stp: 3 mol of gas. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The molar volume at STP is the quickest route from moles of gas to litres: one number, one multiplication, and the volume follows. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

What volume does 3 mol of an ideal gas occupy at standard temperature and pressure (STP: 0 C, 1 atm), where one mole occupies 22.414 L? Give the answer in litres.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the relation: volume V equals moles n times the molar volume at STP, V = n x 22.414 L/mol.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: n = 3.0 mol, molar volume = 22.414 L/mol.
  3. Step 3: Multiply: V = 3.0 x 22.414 = 67.24 L.
  4. Step 4: Check the units: moles times litres per mole leaves litres; check the size: one mole is about 22 L, so 3.0 moles is about 66 L, and 67.24 L matches.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The gas occupies 67.24 L at STP. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same multiplication gives the STP volume of any amount of any ideal gas. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for an ideal gas at exactly 0 C and 1 atm (the traditional STP). Real gases deviate slightly, and the modern IUPAC STP of 0 C and 1 bar (100 kPa) gives a molar volume of 22.711 L/mol instead; at other temperatures or pressures use the ideal gas law directly. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One multiplication, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

At fixed temperature and pressure, the volume of an ideal gas is proportional to the number of moles, with the constant of proportionality (the molar volume) fixed by the gas law at STP. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'moles': 3.0, 'molar_volume_L': 22.414}
def solve(P):
    volume_L = P['moles'] * P['molar_volume_L']
    approx = P['moles'] * 22.0
    return {'volume_L': volume_L, 'approx': approx}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Moles of gas (mol):', P['moles'])
print('Molar volume at STP (L/mol):', P['molar_volume_L'])
print('Volume at STP (L): %.2f' % R['volume_L'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Gas volume at STP: 3 mol of gas """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'moles': 3.0, 'molar_volume_L': 22.414}
    def solve(self, P):
        volume_L = P['moles'] * P['molar_volume_L']
        approx = P['moles'] * 22.0
        return {'volume_L': volume_L, 'approx': approx}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Moles of gas (mol):', P['moles'])
        print('Molar volume at STP (L/mol):', P['molar_volume_L'])
        print('Volume at STP (L): %.2f' % R['volume_L'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

History

Avogadro's hypothesis (1811) that equal volumes of gases hold equal numbers of molecules led to the molar volume; the modern STP value of 22.414 L/mol follows from the ideal gas law at 0 C and 1 atm. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

What volume does 3 mol of an ideal gas occupy at standard temperature and pressure (STP: 0 C, 1 atm), where one mole occupies 22.414 L? Give the answer in litres.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the relation: volume V equals moles n times the molar volume at STP, V = n x 22.414 L/mol. Step 2: Identify the inputs: n = 3.0 mol, molar volume = 22.414 L/mol. Step 3: Multiply: V = 3.0 x 22.414 = 67.24 L. Step 4: Check the units: moles times litres per mole leaves litres; check the size: one mole is about 22 L, so 3.0 moles is about 66 L, and 67.24 L matches. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Moles stated in the problem; molar volume 22.414 L/mol at 0 C and 1 atm.

Solving the ideal gas law for moles is the workhorse gas calculation: pressure, volume and temperature in, amount of substance out.
