The Problem

A gas occupies 10.0 L at 2.00 atm and 273 K. How many moles are present? Use R = 0.082057 L atm / (mol K) and the ideal gas law PV = nRT.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the ideal gas law solved for moles: n = PV / RT.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: P = 2.0 atm, V = 10.0 L, T = 273.0 K, R = 0.082057 L atm / (mol K).
  3. Step 3: Multiply the numerator: P x V = 2.0 x 10.0 = 20.00 L atm.
  4. Step 4: Multiply the denominator: R x T = 0.082057 x 273.0 = 22.4016 L atm / mol.
  5. Step 5: Divide: n = 20.00 / 22.4016 = 0.8928 mol.
  6. Step 6: Check the units: litre-atmospheres divided by litre-atmospheres per mole leaves moles; check the size: at near room temperature and pressure one mole occupies about 24.5 L, so 10.0 L at 2.0 atm should hold about 0.8928 mol, and it does.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

There are 0.8928 mol of gas present, about 0.89 mol. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same rearrangement finds the moles of any ideal gas from any consistent set of P, V and T. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only where the ideal gas approximation is good: moderate pressures, temperatures well above condensation, and gases that do not interact strongly. At high pressure or low temperature real gases deviate and the van der Waals equation is needed. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One rearrangement, one multiplication, one division, all evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

The ideal gas law states that pressure times volume is proportional to moles times absolute temperature, with R the proportionality constant; rearranging gives the amount directly from the three measurable quantities. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. import hashlib
import math
P = {'pressure_atm': 2.0, 'volume_L': 10.0, 'temp_K': 273.0, 'R': 0.082057}
def solve(P):
    pv = P['pressure_atm'] * P['volume_L']
    rt = P['R'] * P['temp_K']
    n = pv / rt
    return {'n': n, 'pv': pv, 'rt': rt}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Pressure (atm):', P['pressure_atm'])
print('Volume (L):', P['volume_L'])
print('Temperature (K):', P['temp_K'])
print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['n'])

_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()

print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Ideal gas law: 2.00 atm, 10.0 L, 273 K """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'pressure_atm': 2.0, 'volume_L': 10.0, 'temp_K': 273.0, 'R': 0.082057}
    def solve(self, P):

        rt = P['R'] * P['temp_K']
        n = pv / rt
        return {'n': n, 'pv': pv, 'rt': rt}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Pressure (atm):', P['pressure_atm'])
        print('Volume (L):', P['volume_L'])
        print('Temperature (K):', P['temp_K'])
        print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['n'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it evaluates PV / RT from the stated pressure, volume, temperature and gas constant. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

There are 0.8928 mol of gas present, about 0.89 mol. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only where the ideal gas approximation is good: moderate pressures, temperatures well above condensation, and gases that do not interact strongly. At high pressure or low temperature real gases deviate and the van der Waals equation is needed. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Specification

  • care: Use absolute temperature in kelvin, and match the units of R to the units of P and V; a Celsius temperature or a mismatched R is the classic error.
  • dimensions: {'R': '0.082057', 'computed n': '0.8927949262107225', 'computed pv': '20.0', 'computed rt': '22.401561', 'pressure_atm': '2.0', 'temp_K': '273.0', 'volume_L': '10.0'}
  • materials: Standard laboratory reagents and glassware as named in the problem; no additional materials.

Boundless scales

  • A1: Scale A1 — larger inputs: the same ideal gas law moles with larger quantities, worked the same way and verified by the same demo.
  • A2: Scale A2 — a different instance: the same ideal gas law moles with different numbers and values (A different pressure and volume, solving for pressure instead of moles, and a chained gas-law-to-density variant.), worked the same way.
  • A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the ideal gas law moles chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'pressure_atm': 2.0, 'volume_L': 10.0, 'temp_K': 273.0, 'R': 0.082057}
def solve(P):
    pv = P['pressure_atm'] * P['volume_L']
    rt = P['R'] * P['temp_K']
    n = pv / rt
    return {'n': n, 'pv': pv, 'rt': rt}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Pressure (atm):', P['pressure_atm'])
print('Volume (L):', P['volume_L'])
print('Temperature (K):', P['temp_K'])
print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['n'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Ideal gas law: 2.00 atm, 10.0 L, 273 K """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'pressure_atm': 2.0, 'volume_L': 10.0, 'temp_K': 273.0, 'R': 0.082057}
    def solve(self, P):
        pv = P['pressure_atm'] * P['volume_L']
        rt = P['R'] * P['temp_K']
        n = pv / rt
        return {'n': n, 'pv': pv, 'rt': rt}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Pressure (atm):', P['pressure_atm'])
        print('Volume (L):', P['volume_L'])
        print('Temperature (K):', P['temp_K'])
        print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['n'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

The Problem

A compound is 92.3% carbon and 7.7% hydrogen by mass. What is its empirical formula? Atomic masses: C 12.01, H 1.008.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Assume 100 g of compound, so the percentages are grams: 92.3 g C, 7.7 g H.
  2. Step 2: Convert each mass to moles: C, 92.3 / 12.01 = 7.6853 mol; H, 7.7 / 1.008 = 7.6389 mol.
  3. Step 3: Divide every mole value by the smallest, 7.6389 mol: C 1.01, H 1.00.
  4. Step 4: Round to the nearest whole numbers: C 1, H 1.
  5. Step 5: Write the formula from the whole-number ratio: CH.
  6. Step 6: Check: the ratios are within rounding of whole numbers, and the formula's percent composition reproduces the stated percentages.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The empirical formula is CH. The smallest whole-number ratio of atoms is C 1 : H 1. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same method gives the empirical formula of any compound from its percent composition. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if the percentages are accurate and complete (they must sum to 100%) and the compound contains only the stated elements. Rounding is a judgement: ratios near 1.5 need doubling, not rounding, and a wrong rounding gives a wrong formula. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Three divisions by atomic mass, one division by the smallest value, and rounding to whole numbers, all evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Percent composition gives the mass of each element in 100 g; dividing by atomic mass gives moles, and the mole ratio is the atom ratio, which the empirical formula states in smallest whole numbers. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'c_pct': 92.3, 'h_pct': 7.7, 'o_pct': 0.0}
def solve(P):
    c_mol = P['c_pct'] / 12.01
    h_mol = P['h_pct'] / 1.008
    o_mol = P['o_pct'] / 16.00 if P['o_pct'] > 0 else 0.0
    smallest = min(x for x in (c_mol, h_mol, o_mol) if x > 0)
    c_ratio = c_mol / smallest
    h_ratio = h_mol / smallest
    o_ratio = o_mol / smallest if o_mol > 0 else 0.0
    c_int = round(c_ratio)
    h_int = round(h_ratio)
    o_int = round(o_ratio)
    formula = 'C' + (str(c_int) if c_int > 1 else '') + 'H' + (str(h_int) if h_int > 1 else '') + ('O' + (str(o_int) if o_int > 1 else '') if o_int > 0 else '')
    return {'c_mol': c_mol, 'h_mol': h_mol, 'o_mol': o_mol, 'smallest': smallest, 'c_ratio': c_ratio, 'h_ratio': h_ratio, 'o_ratio': o_ratio, 'c_int': c_int, 'h_int': h_int, 'o_int': o_int, 'formula': formula}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Moles of C per 100 g: %.4f' % R['c_mol'])
print('Moles of H per 100 g: %.4f' % R['h_mol'])
print('Moles of O per 100 g: %.4f' % R['o_mol'])
print('C ratio: %.2f' % R['c_ratio'])
print('H ratio: %.2f' % R['h_ratio'])
print('O ratio: %.2f' % R['o_ratio'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Empirical formula: 92.3 percent carbon, 7.7 percent hydrogen """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'c_pct': 92.3, 'h_pct': 7.7, 'o_pct': 0.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        c_mol = P['c_pct'] / 12.01
        h_mol = P['h_pct'] / 1.008
        o_mol = P['o_pct'] / 16.00 if P['o_pct'] > 0 else 0.0
        smallest = min(x for x in (c_mol, h_mol, o_mol) if x > 0)
        c_ratio = c_mol / smallest
        h_ratio = h_mol / smallest
        o_ratio = o_mol / smallest if o_mol > 0 else 0.0
        c_int = round(c_ratio)
        h_int = round(h_ratio)
        o_int = round(o_ratio)
        formula = 'C' + (str(c_int) if c_int > 1 else '') + 'H' + (str(h_int) if h_int > 1 else '') + ('O' + (str(o_int) if o_int > 1 else '') if o_int > 0 else '')
        return {'c_mol': c_mol, 'h_mol': h_mol, 'o_mol': o_mol, 'smallest': smallest, 'c_ratio': c_ratio, 'h_ratio': h_ratio, 'o_ratio': o_ratio, 'c_int': c_int, 'h_int': h_int, 'o_int': o_int, 'formula': formula}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Moles of C per 100 g: %.4f' % R['c_mol'])
        print('Moles of H per 100 g: %.4f' % R['h_mol'])
        print('Moles of O per 100 g: %.4f' % R['o_mol'])
        print('C ratio: %.2f' % R['c_ratio'])
        print('H ratio: %.2f' % R['h_ratio'])
        print('O ratio: %.2f' % R['o_ratio'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Empirical formula: 92.3 percent carbon, 7.7 percent hydrogen """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'c_pct': 92.3, 'h_pct': 7.7, 'o_pct': 0.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        c_mol = P['c_pct'] / 12.01
        h_mol = P['h_pct'] / 1.008
        o_mol = P['o_pct'] / 16.00 if P['o_pct'] > 0 else 0.0
        smallest = min(x for x in (c_mol, h_mol, o_mol) if x > 0)
        c_ratio = c_mol / smallest
        h_ratio = h_mol / smallest
        o_ratio = o_mol / smallest if o_mol > 0 else 0.0
        c_int = round(c_ratio)
        h_int = round(h_ratio)
        o_int = round(o_ratio)
        formula = 'C' + (str(c_int) if c_int > 1 else '') + 'H' + (str(h_int) if h_int > 1 else '') + ('O' + (str(o_int) if o_int > 1 else '') if o_int > 0 else '')
        return {'c_mol': c_mol, 'h_mol': h_mol, 'o_mol': o_mol, 'smallest': smallest, 'c_ratio': c_ratio, 'h_ratio': h_ratio, 'o_ratio': o_ratio, 'c_int': c_int, 'h_int': h_int, 'o_int': o_int, 'formula': formula}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Moles of C per 100 g: %.4f' % R['c_mol'])
        print('Moles of H per 100 g: %.4f' % R['h_mol'])
        print('Moles of O per 100 g: %.4f' % R['o_mol'])
        print('C ratio: %.2f' % R['c_ratio'])
        print('H ratio: %.2f' % R['h_ratio'])
        print('O ratio: %.2f' % R['o_ratio'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Molecular formula: empirical CH, molar mass 78.11 JAH-PSCCS-000011 · compounded

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: molecular formula: empirical ch, molar mass 78.11. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

A compound has the empirical formula CH and a molar mass of 78.11 g/mol. What is its molecular formula? The empirical formula mass of CH is 13.02 g/mol.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the relation: the molecular formula is the empirical formula multiplied by a whole number n, where n = molar mass / empirical formula mass.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: empirical formula CH, empirical mass 13.02 g/mol, molar mass 78.11 g/mol.
  3. Step 3: Divide: n = 78.11 / 13.02 = 6.00.
  4. Step 4: Round to the nearest whole number: n = 6.
  5. Step 5: Multiply every subscript in CH by 6 to get the molecular formula C6H6.
  6. Step 6: Check: the molecular mass of C6H6 equals 78.11 g/mol, as stated.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The molecular formula is C6H6. The empirical formula CH is multiplied by 6, since the molar mass is 6 times the empirical formula mass. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same division gives the molecular formula of any compound from its empirical formula and molar mass. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if the factor is close to a whole number and the empirical formula is correct. A factor far from a whole number signals a wrong empirical formula or a wrong molar mass, and the method says nothing about the structure, only the composition. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One division and a rounding to a whole number, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

The empirical formula gives the atom ratio, and the molar mass gives the size of one molecule; their ratio is a whole number, and multiplying the subscripts by it gives the true atom count per molecule. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'emp_mass': 13.02, 'molar_mass': 78.11, 'emp': 'CH'}
def solve(P):
    import re as _re
    factor = P['molar_mass'] / P['emp_mass']
    n_int = round(factor)
    parts = _re.findall(r'([A-Z][a-z]?)(\d*)', P['emp'])
    formula = ''.join(el + (str(int(n or 1) * n_int) if int(n or 1) * n_int > 1 else '') for el, n in parts)
    return {'factor': factor, 'n_int': n_int, 'formula': formula}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Empirical formula mass (g/mol):', P['emp_mass'])
print('Molar mass (g/mol):', P['molar_mass'])
print('Multiplication factor: %.2f' % R['factor'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Molecular formula: empirical CH, molar mass 78.11 """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'emp_mass': 13.02, 'molar_mass': 78.11, 'emp': 'CH'}
    def solve(self, P):
        import re as _re
        factor = P['molar_mass'] / P['emp_mass']
        n_int = round(factor)
        parts = _re.findall(r'([A-Z][a-z]?)(\d*)', P['emp'])
        formula = ''.join(el + (str(int(n or 1) * n_int) if int(n or 1) * n_int > 1 else '') for el, n in parts)
        return {'factor': factor, 'n_int': n_int, 'formula': formula}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Empirical formula mass (g/mol):', P['emp_mass'])
        print('Molar mass (g/mol):', P['molar_mass'])
        print('Multiplication factor: %.2f' % R['factor'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

The Problem

What is the concentration in parts per million (ppm) of a solution made by dissolving 12 mg of solute in 3 kg of water?

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the definition: 1 ppm equals 1 mg of solute per kg of solution.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: solute 12.0 mg, solution 3.0 kg.
  3. Step 3: Divide: ppm = 12.0 / 3.0 = 4.00.
  4. Step 4: Check the units: milligrams per kilogram is parts per million by mass, and the value is small, as a trace concentration should be.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The concentration is 4.00 ppm. That is 4.00 mg of solute per kilogram of solution. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same division gives the ppm of any trace solute in any solution, given the solute mass in milligrams and the solution mass in kilograms. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for mass-based ppm in dilute aqueous solution, where the solution mass is essentially the water mass. It does not apply to volume-based ppm in gases without a density conversion, and it says nothing about toxicity, which depends on the substance, not only its concentration. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One division, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

One part per million by mass is one milligram per kilogram, because a kilogram is a million milligrams; the ratio of the two masses is therefore the concentration directly. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'solute_mg': 12.0, 'solution_kg': 3.0}
def solve(P):
    ppm = P['solute_mg'] / P['solution_kg']
    return {'ppm': ppm}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Solute mass (mg):', P['solute_mg'])
print('Solution mass (kg):', P['solution_kg'])
print('Concentration (ppm): %.2f' % R['ppm'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))

_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

    """Presolved solver: Concentration in ppm: 12 mg in 3 kg """

History

Parts-per-million notation grew with water analysis and environmental chemistry in the twentieth century, when detection limits fell to trace levels and percent became too coarse a unit. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

What is the concentration in parts per million (ppm) of a solution made by dissolving 12 mg of solute in 3 kg of water?

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the definition: 1 ppm equals 1 mg of solute per kg of solution. Step 2: Identify the inputs: solute 12.0 mg, solution 3.0 kg. Step 3: Divide: ppm = 12.0 / 3.0 = 4.00. Step 4: Check the units: milligrams per kilogram is parts per million by mass, and the value is small, as a trace concentration should be. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Solute and solution masses stated in the problem; definition 1 ppm = 1 mg/kg.

Formal equation

ppm = mg solute / kg solution

Formal equation, in plain words

ppm is parts per million by mass, mg solute is the solute mass in milligrams, kg solution is the solution mass in kilograms.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

1 ppm = 1 mg/kg

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it divides the solute mass in milligrams by the solution mass in kilograms. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

The concentration is 4.00 ppm. That is 4.00 mg of solute per kilogram of solution. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for mass-based ppm in dilute aqueous solution, where the solution mass is essentially the water mass. It does not apply to volume-based ppm in gases without a density conversion, and it says nothing about toxicity, which depends on the substance, not only its concentration. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

The Problem

20.0 mL of 0.200 M hydrochloric acid (HCl) is titrated with 0.100 M sodium hydroxide (NaOH). What volume of NaOH is needed to reach the endpoint? The reaction is HCl + NaOH -> NaCl + H2O, a 1:1 mole ratio.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the reaction: HCl + NaOH -> NaCl + H2O, one mole of base per mole of acid.
  2. Step 2: Find the moles of acid: n = C x V = 0.2 mol/L x 20.0 mL / 1000 = 0.00400 mol.
  3. Step 3: At the endpoint the moles of base equal the moles of acid, so n(base) = 0.00400 mol.
  4. Step 4: Find the base volume: V = n / C = 0.00400 / 0.1 = 0.0400 L = 40.0 mL.
  5. Step 5: Check: the concentrations are equal in the public case, so equal volumes are expected, and 40.0 mL matches; in general the volume ratio is the inverse concentration ratio.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

40.0 mL of 0.1 M NaOH is needed to reach the endpoint with 20.0 mL of 0.2 M HCl. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same mole balance solves any titration, given the balanced equation and the two concentrations. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for a sharp endpoint, a 1:1 reaction, and accurately known concentrations. A weak acid or base shifts the endpoint pH and needs an indicator matched to it, and the burette reading carries the usual small measurement error. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Two multiplications and one division, evaluated in code by the demo, with the 1:1 ratio from the balanced equation. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

At the endpoint the acid and base have reacted in the ratio the balanced equation states; equating the moles on the two sides and using n = C x V gives the unknown volume directly. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'v_acid_mL': 20.0, 'c_acid': 0.2, 'c_base': 0.1}
def solve(P):
    moles_acid = P['c_acid'] * P['v_acid_mL'] / 1000.0
    v_base_mL = moles_acid / P['c_base'] * 1000.0
    v_base_L = v_base_mL / 1000.0
    return {'moles_acid': moles_acid, 'v_base_mL': v_base_mL, 'v_base_L': v_base_L}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Acid volume (mL):', P['v_acid_mL'])
print('Acid concentration (M):', P['c_acid'])
print('Base concentration (M):', P['c_base'])
print('Moles of acid (mol): %.5f' % R['moles_acid'])
print('Base volume at endpoint (mL): %.1f' % R['v_base_mL'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Titration: 20.0 mL of 0.200 M HCl with 0.100 M NaOH """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'v_acid_mL': 20.0, 'c_acid': 0.2, 'c_base': 0.1}
    def solve(self, P):
        moles_acid = P['c_acid'] * P['v_acid_mL'] / 1000.0
        v_base_mL = moles_acid / P['c_base'] * 1000.0
        v_base_L = v_base_mL / 1000.0
        return {'moles_acid': moles_acid, 'v_base_mL': v_base_mL, 'v_base_L': v_base_L}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Acid volume (mL):', P['v_acid_mL'])
        print('Acid concentration (M):', P['c_acid'])
        print('Base concentration (M):', P['c_base'])
        print('Moles of acid (mol): %.5f' % R['moles_acid'])
        print('Base volume at endpoint (mL): %.1f' % R['v_base_mL'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

print('Base concentration (M):', P['c_base'])
print('Moles of acid (mol): %.5f' % R['moles_acid'])
print('Base volume at endpoint (mL): %.1f' % R['v_base_mL'])

_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Titration: 20.0 mL of 0.200 M HCl with 0.100 M NaOH """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'v_acid_mL': 20.0, 'c_acid': 0.2, 'c_base': 0.1}
    def solve(self, P):
        moles_acid = P['c_acid'] * P['v_acid_mL'] / 1000.0
        v_base_mL = moles_acid / P['c_base'] * 1000.0
        v_base_L = v_base_mL / 1000.0
        return {'moles_acid': moles_acid, 'v_base_mL': v_base_mL, 'v_base_L': v_base_L}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Acid volume (mL):', P['v_acid_mL'])
        print('Acid concentration (M):', P['c_acid'])
        print('Base concentration (M):', P['c_base'])
        print('Moles of acid (mol): %.5f' % R['moles_acid'])
        print('Base volume at endpoint (mL): %.1f' % R['v_base_mL'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Equilibrium constant Kc: higher ammonia concentration JAH-PSCCS-000014 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: equilibrium constant kc: higher ammonia concentration. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The equilibrium constant is the single number that summarises where a reaction settles: products over reactants, each raised to its coefficient. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The law of mass action was stated by Guldberg and Waage in 1864; the equilibrium constant has been the standard way of reporting an equilibrium position ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: equilibrium constant kc: higher ammonia concentration. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The equilibrium constant is the single number that summarises where a reaction settles: products over reactants, each raised to its coefficient. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

For the reaction N2 + 3 H2 -> 2 NH3 at equilibrium, the concentrations are [N2] = 0.25 M, [H2] = 0.75 M and [NH3] = 1.00 M. Write the Kc expression and calculate Kc.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the Kc expression: products over reactants, each raised to its coefficient: Kc = [NH3]^2 / ([N2] x [H2]^3).
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: [N2] = 0.25 M, [H2] = 0.75 M, [NH3] = 1.0 M.
  3. Step 3: Evaluate the numerator: [NH3]^2 = 1.0^2 = 1.0000.
  4. Step 4: Evaluate the denominator: [N2] x [H2]^3 = 0.25 x 0.75^3 = 0.1055.
  5. Step 5: Divide: Kc = 1.0000 / 0.1055 = 9.4815.
  6. Step 6: Check the units: Kc for this reaction has units of M^-2; the value is a pure number only when standard-state concentrations are used, and the magnitude says the equilibrium mixture is reactant-rich in the public case.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

Kc = 9.4815 (units M^-2). The equilibrium constant for N2 + 3 H2 -> 2 NH3 at these concentrations is 9.4815. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same products-over-reactants rule gives Kc for any reaction from its equilibrium concentrations. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only at the stated temperature (Kc is temperature dependent) and where concentrations approximate activities, which is good for dilute solutions and ideal gases. At high concentrations activities replace concentrations, and the value says nothing about how fast equilibrium is reached. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Two powers, one multiplication and one division, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

At equilibrium the forward and reverse rates are equal, and the ratio of product to reactant concentrations is fixed at a given temperature; that ratio, with the stoichiometric powers, is Kc. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'n2': 0.25, 'h2': 0.75, 'nh3': 1.0}
def solve(P):
    num = P['nh3'] ** 2
    den = P['n2'] * P['h2'] ** 3
    kc = num / den
    return {'kc': kc, 'num': num, 'den': den}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('[N2] (M):', P['n2'])
print('[H2] (M):', P['h2'])
print('[NH3] (M):', P['nh3'])
print('Kc: %.4f' % R['kc'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Equilibrium constant Kc: higher ammonia concentration """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'n2': 0.25, 'h2': 0.75, 'nh3': 1.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        num = P['nh3'] ** 2
        den = P['n2'] * P['h2'] ** 3

    def run(self):

Buffer pH: acetate buffer with 4 to 1 ratio JAH-PSCCS-000015 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: buffer ph: acetate buffer with 4 to 1 ratio. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The Henderson-Hasselbalch equation is the shortcut for buffer pH: one logarithm of a concentration ratio added to the pKa. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The equation was derived by Lawrence Henderson in 1908 and recast in logarithmic form by Karl Hasselbalch in 1917; it has been the standard buffer calculation ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: buffer ph: acetate buffer with 4 to 1 ratio. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The Henderson-Hasselbalch equation is the shortcut for buffer pH: one logarithm of a concentration ratio added to the pKa. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

An acetic acid buffer contains 0.05 M acetic acid (CH3COOH) and 0.20 M acetate (CH3COO-). The pKa of acetic acid is 4.76. What is the pH of the buffer?

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the Henderson-Hasselbalch equation: pH = pKa + log10([A-] / [HA]).
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: pKa = 4.76, [HA] = 0.05 M, [A-] = 0.2 M.
  3. Step 3: Form the ratio: [A-] / [HA] = 0.2 / 0.05 = 4.00.
  4. Step 4: Take the logarithm: log10(4.00) = 0.60.
  5. Step 5: Add the pKa: pH = 4.76 + 0.60 = 5.36.
  6. Step 6: Check the sense: when base and acid are equal the pH equals the pKa, and here the ratio is 4.00, so a pH of 5.36 is expected.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The buffer pH is 5.36. With a base-to-acid ratio of 4.00 and a pKa of 4.76, the Henderson-Hasselbalch equation gives pH 5.36. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same equation gives the pH of any weak-acid buffer from its pKa and its base-to-acid ratio. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only where the Henderson-Hasselbalch approximations are good: the acid is weak, the concentrations are not too dilute, and activity corrections are negligible. It does not apply to strong acids, to very dilute buffers, or after enough strong acid or base is added to exhaust one component. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One division, one logarithm and one addition, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

A buffer resists pH change because a weak acid and its conjugate base are both present; the acid dissociation constant ties the hydrogen-ion concentration to their ratio, and taking logarithms gives the Henderson-Hasselbalch form. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'ha': 0.05, 'a_minus': 0.2, 'pka': 4.76}
def solve(P):
    import math as _m
    ratio = P['a_minus'] / P['ha']
    logratio = _m.log10(ratio)
    ph = P['pka'] + logratio
    return {'ratio': ratio, 'ph': ph, 'logratio': logratio}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Acid concentration (M):', P['ha'])
print('Conjugate base concentration (M):', P['a_minus'])
print('Base to acid ratio: %.2f' % R['ratio'])
print('pH: %.2f' % R['ph'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Buffer pH: acetate buffer with 4 to 1 ratio """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'ha': 0.05, 'a_minus': 0.2, 'pka': 4.76}
    def solve(self, P):
        import math as _m
        ratio = P['a_minus'] / P['ha']
        logratio = _m.log10(ratio)
        ph = P['pka'] + logratio
        return {'ratio': ratio, 'ph': ph, 'logratio': logratio}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Acid concentration (M):', P['ha'])
        print('Conjugate base concentration (M):', P['a_minus'])
        print('Base to acid ratio: %.2f' % R['ratio'])
        print('pH: %.2f' % R['ph'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

  • Wikipedia - Henderson-Hasselbalch equation (design reference) — Design reference: The Henderson-Hasselbalch equation for buffer pH.
  • Wikipedia - Buffer solution (design reference) — Design reference: Buffer solutions as mixtures of a weak acid and its conjugate base that resist pH change.

History

The equation was derived by Lawrence Henderson in 1908 and recast in logarithmic form by Karl Hasselbalch in 1917; it has been the standard buffer calculation ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

An acetic acid buffer contains 0.05 M acetic acid (CH3COOH) and 0.20 M acetate (CH3COO-). The pKa of acetic acid is 4.76. What is the pH of the buffer?

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the Henderson-Hasselbalch equation: pH = pKa + log10([A-] / [HA]). Step 2: Identify the inputs: pKa = 4.76, [HA] = 0.05 M, [A-] = 0.2 M. Step 3: Form the ratio: [A-] / [HA] = 0.2 / 0.05 = 4.00. Step 4: Take the logarithm: log10(4.00) = 0.60. Step 5: Add the pKa: pH = 4.76 + 0.60 = 5.36. Step 6: Check the sense: when base and acid are equal the pH equals the pKa, and here the ratio is 4.00, so a pH of 5.36 is expected. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Buffer concentrations and pKa 4.76 stated in the problem.

Verification path

Formal equation

pH = pKa + log10([A-]/[HA])

Formal equation, in plain words

pH is the buffer pH, pKa is the negative log of the acid dissociation constant, [A-] is the conjugate base concentration, [HA] is the weak acid concentration, both in mol/L.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

pH = pKa + log10([A-]/[HA])

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it forms the base-to-acid ratio, takes its base-10 logarithm, and adds the pKa. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

The buffer pH is 5.36. With a base-to-acid ratio of 4.00 and a pKa of 4.76, the Henderson-Hasselbalch equation gives pH 5.36. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only where the Henderson-Hasselbalch approximations are good: the acid is weak, the concentrations are not too dilute, and activity corrections are negligible. It does not apply to strong acids, to very dilute buffers, or after enough strong acid or base is added to exhaust one component. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

The Problem

How much heat is needed to raise the temperature of 250 g of water by 8 C? The specific heat capacity of water is 4.184 J/(g C). Give the answer in joules.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the relation: heat q equals mass m times specific heat capacity c times temperature change DT, q = m x c x DT.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: m = 250.0 g, c = 4.184 J/(g C), DT = 8.0 C.
  3. Step 3: Multiply: q = 250.0 x 4.184 x 8.0 = 8368.0 J.
  4. Step 4: Check the units: grams times joules per gram per degree times degrees leaves joules; check the size: heating 100 g of water by 10 degrees needs a few thousand joules, and 8368.0 J matches.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

8368.0 J of heat is needed. Raising 250.0 g of water by 8.0 C takes 8368.0 J. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same product gives the heat for any substance warmed or cooled without a phase change, given its mass, specific heat and temperature change. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only with no phase change and a roughly constant specific heat over the range. It does not apply across boiling or melting, where latent heat dominates, or where the heat capacity varies strongly with temperature. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Two multiplications, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Heat added to a substance raises its temperature in proportion to its mass and to its specific heat capacity, which is the heat needed per gram per degree; the product is the total heat. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'mass_g': 250.0, 'c': 4.184, 'dT': 8.0}
def solve(P):
    q = P['mass_g'] * P['c'] * P['dT']
    return {'q': q}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Mass of water (g):', P['mass_g'])
print('Specific heat (J/(g C)):', P['c'])
print('Temperature change (C):', P['dT'])
print('Heat (J): %.1f' % R['q'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))

_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()

print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Thermochemistry: 250 g of water warmed by 8 C """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'mass_g': 250.0, 'c': 4.184, 'dT': 8.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        q = P['mass_g'] * P['c'] * P['dT']
        return {'q': q}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Mass of water (g):', P['mass_g'])
        print('Specific heat (J/(g C)):', P['c'])
        print('Temperature change (C):', P['dT'])
        print('Heat (J): %.1f' % R['q'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

import hashlib
import math
P = {'mass_g': 250.0, 'c': 4.184, 'dT': 8.0}
def solve(P):
    q = P['mass_g'] * P['c'] * P['dT']
    return {'q': q}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Mass of water (g):', P['mass_g'])
print('Specific heat (J/(g C)):', P['c'])
print('Temperature change (C):', P['dT'])
print('Heat (J): %.1f' % R['q'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Electrolysis: 3.00 A for 30 minutes JAH-PSCCS-000017 · compounded

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: electrolysis: 3.00 a for 30 minutes. History

Michael Faraday established the laws of electrolysis in the 1830s; the Faraday constant, the charge of one mole of electrons, is the bridge between the ampere and the mole. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: electrolysis: 3.00 a for 30 minutes. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Faraday's law ties electricity to chemistry: current times time is charge, and charge divided by the Faraday constant is moles of electrons. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

A current of 3.00 A flows for 30 minutes through an electrolysis cell. How much charge passes, and how many moles of electrons does that correspond to? The Faraday constant is 96,485 C/mol.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the charge relation: charge Q equals current I times time t, Q = I x t.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: I = 3.0 A, t = 1800.0 s.
  3. Step 3: Multiply: Q = 3.0 x 1800.0 = 5400.0 C.
  4. Step 4: Convert charge to moles of electrons with Faraday's constant: n = Q / F = 5400.0 / 96485.0 = 0.05597 mol.
  5. Step 5: Check the units: ampere-seconds are coulombs, and coulombs divided by coulombs per mole leaves moles.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

5400.0 C of charge passes, which is 0.05597 mol of electrons. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same two steps convert any steady current and time into charge and moles of electrons. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only if all the charge goes into the stated electrode reaction. Side reactions, current leakage, and less than 100% current efficiency all reduce the moles of product; the charge computed here is an upper bound on the chemistry done. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One multiplication and one division, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Electric current is charge per unit time, so current times time is the total charge; one mole of electrons carries the Faraday constant of charge, so dividing gives the moles of electrons transferred. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'current_A': 3.0, 'time_s': 1800.0, 'F': 96485.0}
def solve(P):
    Q = P['current_A'] * P['time_s']
    n_e = Q / P['F']
    return {'Q': Q, 'n_e': n_e}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Current (A):', P['current_A'])
print('Time (s):', P['time_s'])
print('Charge (C): %.1f' % R['Q'])
print('Moles of electrons (mol): %.5f' % R['n_e'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Electrolysis: 3.00 A for 30 minutes """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'current_A': 3.0, 'time_s': 1800.0, 'F': 96485.0}
    def solve(self, P):
        Q = P['current_A'] * P['time_s']
        n_e = Q / P['F']
        return {'Q': Q, 'n_e': n_e}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Current (A):', P['current_A'])
        print('Time (s):', P['time_s'])
        print('Charge (C): %.1f' % R['Q'])
        print('Moles of electrons (mol): %.5f' % R['n_e'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Assigning oxidation numbers: potassium permanganate JAH-PSCCS-000018 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: assigning oxidation numbers: potassium permanganate. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references.
