Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: assigning oxidation numbers: potassium permanganate. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Oxidation numbers are bookkeeping for electrons: a small set of rules assigns a number to every atom, and the numbers must sum to the charge. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

Assign oxidation numbers to every atom in potassium permanganate, KMnO4, and show that they sum to the charge of the molecule (zero). Rules: K is +1, O is -2.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the rules: potassium is +1, oxygen is -2, and the oxidation numbers in a neutral molecule sum to zero.
  2. Step 2: Let the unknown atom (manganese) have oxidation number x.
  3. Step 3: Add the contributions: 1 x (+1) for potassium, plus x for manganese, plus 4 x (-2) for oxygen, equals 0.
  4. Step 4: Solve: 1 + x - 8 = 0, so x = 7.
  5. Step 5: Assign: K +1, Mn +7, O -2 (x4); the sum is 0, the charge of the neutral molecule, as required.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The oxidation numbers are K +1, Mn +7, O -2. They sum to 0, the charge of neutral KMnO4. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same rules assign oxidation numbers in any molecule or ion, given the formula and the charge. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only under the standard assignment rules, which are conventions, not measurements: peroxides, superoxides, and metal hydrides are exceptions where O is not -2 or H is not +1, and the numbers describe bookkeeping, not real atomic charges. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One linear equation in one unknown, solved and checked by the sum rule, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Oxidation numbers track electron transfer by assigning shared electrons to the more electronegative atom; the assignment rules are definitions, and the sum rule follows from charge conservation. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'h_ox': 1, 'o_ox': -2, 'h_count': 1, 'o_count': 4, 'formula': 'KMnO4'}
def solve(P):
    s_ox = -(P['h_count'] * P['h_ox'] + P['o_count'] * P['o_ox'])
    check = P['h_count'] * P['h_ox'] + s_ox + P['o_count'] * P['o_ox']
    return {'s_ox': s_ox, 'check': check}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Oxidation number of H (or K):', P['h_ox'])
print('Oxidation number of O:', P['o_ox'])
print('Oxidation number of S (or Mn): %d' % R['s_ox'])
print('Sum of oxidation numbers:', R['check'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Assigning oxidation numbers: potassium permanganate """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'h_ox': 1, 'o_ox': -2, 'h_count': 1, 'o_count': 4, 'formula': 'KMnO4'}
    def solve(self, P):
        s_ox = -(P['h_count'] * P['h_ox'] + P['o_count'] * P['o_ox'])
        check = P['h_count'] * P['h_ox'] + s_ox + P['o_count'] * P['o_ox']
        return {'s_ox': s_ox, 'check': check}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Oxidation number of H (or K):', P['h_ox'])
        print('Oxidation number of O:', P['o_ox'])
        print('Oxidation number of S (or Mn): %d' % R['s_ox'])
        print('Sum of oxidation numbers:', R['check'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

History

Oxidation numbers were formalised in the twentieth century as redox chemistry was systematised; the rules used here (H +1, O -2, sum equals charge) are the standard textbook set. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

Assign oxidation numbers to every atom in potassium permanganate, KMnO4, and show that they sum to the charge of the molecule (zero). Rules: K is +1, O is -2.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the rules: potassium is +1, oxygen is -2, and the oxidation numbers in a neutral molecule sum to zero. Step 2: Let the unknown atom (manganese) have oxidation number x. Step 3: Add the contributions: 1 x (+1) for potassium, plus x for manganese, plus 4 x (-2) for oxygen, equals 0. Step 4: Solve: 1 + x - 8 = 0, so x = 7. Step 5: Assign: K +1, Mn +7, O -2 (x4); the sum is 0, the charge of the neutral molecule, as required. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Formula and rules stated in the problem; standard oxidation-number rules.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it solves the sum-to-zero equation for the unknown oxidation number and verifies the sum equals the molecular charge. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Results reported

The oxidation numbers are K +1, Mn +7, O -2. They sum to 0, the charge of neutral KMnO4. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only under the standard assignment rules, which are conventions, not measurements: peroxides, superoxides, and metal hydrides are exceptions where O is not -2 or H is not +1, and the numbers describe bookkeeping, not real atomic charges. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Balancing a redox equation: dichromate and iron(II) JAH-PSCCS-000019 · complex

Balancing a redox equation is electron accounting: split into half-reactions, balance atoms and charge in each, then equalise the electrons. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. History

The half-reaction (ion-electron) method was developed in the early twentieth century and is the standard textbook procedure for redox equations in acidic and basic solution. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. Balancing a redox equation is electron accounting: split into half-reactions, balance atoms and charge in each, then equalise the electrons. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. The Problem

Balance the redox reaction in acidic solution: Cr2O7^2- + Fe2+ -> Cr3+ + Fe3+ + H2O. Give the balanced equation with the smallest whole-number coefficients, and verify atom and charge balance.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Split into half-reactions: reduction Cr2O7^2- -> Cr3+ (chromium goes from +6 to +3, gaining 3 electrons per chromium, 6 in total) and oxidation Fe2+ -> Fe3+ (losing 1 electron).
  2. Step 2: Balance the reduction half-reaction: add 7 H2O to the right for oxygen, then 14 H+ to the left for hydrogen, then 6 electrons to the left for charge: Cr2O7^2- + 14 H+ + 6 e- -> 2 Cr3+ + 7 H2O.
  3. Step 3: Balance the oxidation half-reaction: Fe2+ -> Fe3+ + e-.
  4. Step 4: Equalise the electrons: multiply the oxidation half-reaction by 6, then add: Cr2O7^2- + 6 Fe2+ + 14 H+ -> 2 Cr3+ + 6 Fe3+ + 7 H2O.
  5. Step 5: Verify by counting: Cr 2 = 2, Fe 6 = 6, O 7 = 7, H 14 = 14, charge +24 = +24 on both sides. The demo recounts atoms and charge in code.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The balanced equation is Cr2O7^2- + 6 Fe2+ + 14 H+ -> 2 Cr3+ + 6 Fe3+ + 7 H2O. Atoms and charge balance on both sides. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same half-reaction method balances any redox equation, given the reactants, products, and medium. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for the stated medium (acidic solution); in basic solution the balancing uses OH- instead of H+, and the coefficients differ. The method also assumes the stated oxidation states are correct. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

The half-reaction method, with the balance verified by counting atoms and charge on both sides in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Redox reactions conserve atoms and charge, and electrons lost in oxidation equal electrons gained in reduction; balancing the half-reactions separately enforces both conservations at once. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'ox_coeff': 1, 'fe_coeff': 6, 'h_coeff': 14, 'water_coeff': 7, 'o_per_ox': 7, 'ox_charge': -2, 'metal_count': 2, 'metal_charge': 3}
def solve(P):
    o_left = P['o_per_ox'] * P['ox_coeff']
    h_left = P['h_coeff']
    charge_left = P['ox_charge'] * P['ox_coeff'] + 2 * P['fe_coeff'] + P['h_coeff']
    o_right = P['water_coeff']
    h_right = 2 * P['water_coeff']
    charge_right = P['metal_count'] * P['metal_charge'] + 3 * P['fe_coeff']
    balanced = (o_left == o_right and h_left == h_right and charge_left == charge_right)
    return {'charge_left': charge_left, 'charge_right': charge_right, 'balanced': balanced, 'o_left': o_left, 'h_left': h_left}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('O atoms left:', R['o_left'])
print('H atoms left:', R['h_left'])
print('Charge left:', R['charge_left'])
print('Charge right:', R['charge_right'])
print('Balanced:', R['balanced'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Balancing a redox equation: dichromate and iron(II) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'ox_coeff': 1, 'fe_coeff': 6, 'h_coeff': 14, 'water_coeff': 7, 'o_per_ox': 7, 'ox_charge': -2, 'metal_count': 2, 'metal_charge': 3}
    def solve(self, P):
        o_left = P['o_per_ox'] * P['ox_coeff']
        h_left = P['h_coeff']
        charge_left = P['ox_charge'] * P['ox_coeff'] + 2 * P['fe_coeff'] + P['h_coeff']
        o_right = P['water_coeff']
        h_right = 2 * P['water_coeff']
        charge_right = P['metal_count'] * P['metal_charge'] + 3 * P['fe_coeff']
        balanced = (o_left == o_right and h_left == h_right and charge_left == charge_right)
        return {'charge_left': charge_left, 'charge_right': charge_right, 'balanced': balanced, 'o_left': o_left, 'h_left': h_left}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('O atoms left:', R['o_left'])
        print('H atoms left:', R['h_left'])
        print('Charge left:', R['charge_left'])
        print('Charge right:', R['charge_right'])
        print('Balanced:', R['balanced'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Formal equation

Cr2O7^2- + 6 Fe2+ + 14 H+ -> 2 Cr3+ + 6 Fe3+ + 7 H2O

Formal equation, in plain words

Cr2O7^2- is dichromate, Fe2+ is iron(II), H+ is the acid medium, Cr3+ is chromium(III), Fe3+ is iron(III), H2O is water; coefficients are the smallest whole numbers that balance atoms and charge.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

electrons lost = electrons gained

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it counts O and H atoms and the total charge on each side of the balanced equation and compares them. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

The balanced equation is Cr2O7^2- + 6 Fe2+ + 14 H+ -> 2 Cr3+ + 6 Fe3+ + 7 H2O. Atoms and charge balance on both sides. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for the stated medium (acidic solution); in basic solution the balancing uses OH- instead of H+, and the coefficients differ. The method also assumes the stated oxidation states are correct. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Specification

  • care: Balance in the medium stated; an acidic-solution balance is wrong for a basic solution, and vice versa.
  • dimensions: {'computed charge_left': '24', 'computed charge_right': '24', 'computed h_left': '14', 'computed o_left': '7', 'fe_coeff': '6', 'h_coeff': '14', 'metal_charge': '3', 'metal_count': '2', 'o_per_ox': '7', 'ox_charge': '-2', 'ox_coeff': '1', 'water_coeff': '7'}
  • materials: Standard laboratory reagents and glassware as named in the problem; no additional materials.

Boundless scales

  • A1: Scale A1 — larger inputs: the same redox balancing with larger quantities, worked the same way and verified by the same demo.
  • A2: Scale A2 — a different instance: the same redox balancing with different numbers and values (A different oxidising agent, balancing in basic solution, and a chained balance-to-stoichiometry variant.), worked the same way.
  • A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the redox balancing chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Key figures

  • charge_right: 24

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'ox_coeff': 1, 'fe_coeff': 6, 'h_coeff': 14, 'water_coeff': 7, 'o_per_ox': 7, 'ox_charge': -2, 'metal_count': 2, 'metal_charge': 3}
def solve(P):
    o_left = P['o_per_ox'] * P['ox_coeff']
    h_left = P['h_coeff']
    charge_left = P['ox_charge'] * P['ox_coeff'] + 2 * P['fe_coeff'] + P['h_coeff']
    o_right = P['water_coeff']
    h_right = 2 * P['water_coeff']
    charge_right = P['metal_count'] * P['metal_charge'] + 3 * P['fe_coeff']
    balanced = (o_left == o_right and h_left == h_right and charge_left == charge_right)
    return {'charge_left': charge_left, 'charge_right': charge_right, 'balanced': balanced, 'o_left': o_left, 'h_left': h_left}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('O atoms left:', R['o_left'])
print('H atoms left:', R['h_left'])
print('Charge left:', R['charge_left'])
print('Charge right:', R['charge_right'])
print('Balanced:', R['balanced'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Balancing a redox equation: dichromate and iron(II) """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'ox_coeff': 1, 'fe_coeff': 6, 'h_coeff': 14, 'water_coeff': 7, 'o_per_ox': 7, 'ox_charge': -2, 'metal_count': 2, 'metal_charge': 3}
    def solve(self, P):
        o_left = P['o_per_ox'] * P['ox_coeff']
        h_left = P['h_coeff']
        charge_left = P['ox_charge'] * P['ox_coeff'] + 2 * P['fe_coeff'] + P['h_coeff']
        o_right = P['water_coeff']
        h_right = 2 * P['water_coeff']
        charge_right = P['metal_count'] * P['metal_charge'] + 3 * P['fe_coeff']
        balanced = (o_left == o_right and h_left == h_right and charge_left == charge_right)
        return {'charge_left': charge_left, 'charge_right': charge_right, 'balanced': balanced, 'o_left': o_left, 'h_left': h_left}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('O atoms left:', R['o_left'])
        print('H atoms left:', R['h_left'])
        print('Charge left:', R['charge_left'])
        print('Charge right:', R['charge_right'])
        print('Balanced:', R['balanced'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Rate law: first order in A, first order in B JAH-PSCCS-000020 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: rate law: first order in a, first order in b. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The rate law is found from experiments, not from the equation: doubling one reactant at a time reveals its order, and one rate measurement then fixes k. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The empirical rate law and the method of initial rates were established in nineteenth-century kinetics; the rate constant and its units have been reported this way ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: rate law: first order in a, first order in b. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. The rate law is found from experiments, not from the equation: doubling one reactant at a time reveals its order, and one rate measurement then fixes k. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

For the reaction A + B -> products, experiments show the rate doubles when [A] doubles (first order in A) and doubles when [B] doubles (first order in B). At [A] = 0.10 M and [B] = 0.20 M the rate is 0.012 M/s. What is the rate law and the rate constant k, with units?

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the rate law from the orders: rate = k [A]^1 [B]^1.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: [A] = 0.1 M, [B] = 0.2 M, rate = 0.012 M/s.
  3. Step 3: Rearrange for k: k = rate / ([A]^1 [B]^1).
  4. Step 4: Evaluate the denominator: [A]^1 x [B]^1 = 0.1 x 0.2 = 0.02000 M^2.
  5. Step 5: Divide: k = 0.012 / 0.02000 = 0.60 M^-1 s^-1.
  6. Step 6: Check the units: rate (M/s) divided by M^2 leaves M^-1 s^-1, the correct units for a second-order overall reaction; check the size by substituting back: 0.60 x 0.02000 = 0.012 M/s, as stated.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The rate law is rate = k [A][B], and k = 0.60 M^-1 s^-1. The reaction is first order in A, first order in B, second order overall. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same method gives the rate law and k for any reaction, given the orders and one rate measurement. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only at the stated temperature (k is temperature dependent) and only for the concentration range studied; orders can change with mechanism, and the stoichiometric coefficients do not determine the orders. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One rearrangement and one division, evaluated in code by the demo, with a substitution check at the end. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Reaction rates depend on concentrations through powers (orders) determined experimentally; the rate law summarises those powers, and k is the proportionality constant at the stated temperature. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'order_a': 1, 'order_b': 1, 'a_conc': 0.1, 'b_conc': 0.2, 'rate': 0.012}
def solve(P):
    den = P['a_conc'] ** P['order_a'] * P['b_conc'] ** P['order_b']
    k = P['rate'] / den
    return {'k': k, 'den': den}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Order in A:', P['order_a'])
print('Order in B:', P['order_b'])
print('Rate (M/s):', P['rate'])
print('Rate constant k: %.4g' % R['k'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Rate constant k: 0.6

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Rate law: first order in A, first order in B """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'order_a': 1, 'order_b': 1, 'a_conc': 0.1, 'b_conc': 0.2, 'rate': 0.012}
    def solve(self, P):
        den = P['a_conc'] ** P['order_a'] * P['b_conc'] ** P['order_b']
        k = P['rate'] / den
        return {'k': k, 'den': den}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Order in A:', P['order_a'])
        print('Order in B:', P['order_b'])
        print('Rate (M/s):', P['rate'])
        print('Rate constant k: %.4g' % R['k'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Rate law: first order in A, first order in B """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'order_a': 1, 'order_b': 1, 'a_conc': 0.1, 'b_conc': 0.2, 'rate': 0.012}
    def solve(self, P):
        den = P['a_conc'] ** P['order_a'] * P['b_conc'] ** P['order_b']
        k = P['rate'] / den
        return {'k': k, 'den': den}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Order in A:', P['order_a'])
        print('Order in B:', P['order_b'])
        print('Rate (M/s):', P['rate'])
        print('Rate constant k: %.4g' % R['k'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Half-life: 200 g after 4 half-lives JAH-PSCCS-000021 · simple

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: half-life: 200 g after 4 half-lives. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Half-life arithmetic is repeated halving: each half-life cuts what is left in half, and n half-lives multiply by one half n times. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

Half-life was introduced with the study of radioactivity around 1900 (Rutherford, Soddy); the exponential decay law it expresses is the standard description of first-order decay. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: half-life: 200 g after 4 half-lives. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Half-life arithmetic is repeated halving: each half-life cuts what is left in half, and n half-lives multiply by one half n times. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

A sample of 200 g of a radioactive substance has a half-life of 1 hour. What mass remains after 4 half-lives (4 hours)?

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the half-life relation: after each half-life the mass halves, so after n half-lives m = m0 x (1/2)^n.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: m0 = 200.0 g, n = 4.
  3. Step 3: Evaluate the factor: (1/2)^4 = 0.0625.
  4. Step 4: Multiply: m = 200.0 x 0.0625 = 12.50 g.
  5. Step 5: Check by halving step by step: after 1 half-life 100.0 g, after 2 half-lives 50.0 g, and so on until 12.50 g after 4 half-lives.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

After 4 half-lives, 12.50 g remains of the original 200.0 g. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same halving gives the remaining mass of any first-order decay after any number of half-lives. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for a first-order decay with a constant half-life and no fresh material added. It does not apply to decay chains where a daughter product also decays, or where the half-life itself changes. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One power and one multiplication, evaluated in code by the demo, with a step-by-step halving check. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Radioactive decay is a first-order process: a fixed fraction of the remaining atoms decays per unit time, so the amount halves over each fixed interval, the half-life. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'mass0': 200.0, 'n_half': 4}
def solve(P):
    factor = 0.5 ** P['n_half']
    half1 = P['mass0'] * 0.5
    half2 = P['mass0'] * 0.25
    remaining = P['mass0'] * factor
    return {'remaining': remaining, 'factor': factor, 'half1': half1, 'half2': half2}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Initial mass (g):', P['mass0'])
print('Number of half-lives:', P['n_half'])
print('Mass remaining (g): %.2f' % R['remaining'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Initial mass (g): 200.0
Number of half-lives: 4
Mass remaining (g): 12.50
Run 1 SHA-256: e1eeb4bc1290914aa1b3a8f0bc775d06782b44ad40bb2532c482862c0b5e4f48
Run 2 SHA-256: e1eeb4bc1290914aa1b3a8f0bc775d06782b44ad40bb2532c482862c0b5e4f48
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Half-life: 200 g after 4 half-lives """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'mass0': 200.0, 'n_half': 4}
    def solve(self, P):
        factor = 0.5 ** P['n_half']
        half1 = P['mass0'] * 0.5
        half2 = P['mass0'] * 0.25
        remaining = P['mass0'] * factor
        return {'remaining': remaining, 'factor': factor, 'half1': half1, 'half2': half2}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Initial mass (g):', P['mass0'])
        print('Number of half-lives:', P['n_half'])
        print('Mass remaining (g): %.2f' % R['remaining'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it multiplies the initial mass by one half raised to the number of half-lives. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

        half2 = P['mass0'] * 0.25

        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)

Solubility: barium sulfate Ksp JAH-PSCCS-000022 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: solubility: barium sulfate ksp. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Molar solubility is the square root of the solubility product for a 1:1 salt: one number, one root, and the dissolved amount follows. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The solubility product was introduced with the law of mass action in the nineteenth century; Ksp tables for sparingly soluble salts have been standard reference data ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Description

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: solubility: barium sulfate ksp. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Molar solubility is the square root of the solubility product for a 1:1 salt: one number, one root, and the dissolved amount follows. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

Barium sulfate, BaSO4, has a solubility product Ksp = 1.1 x 10^-10 at 25 C. What is its molar solubility in pure water? BaSO4(s) -> Ba2+ + SO4^2-, so solubility s satisfies Ksp = s^2.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the dissolution: the salt gives one cation and one anion per formula unit, so if s mol/L dissolves, each ion is s mol/L.
  2. Step 2: Write the solubility product: Ksp = [cation] x [anion] = s x s = s^2.
  3. Step 3: Solve: s = sqrt(Ksp) = sqrt(1.1e-10) = 1.049e-05 mol/L.
  4. Step 4: Check the sense: a Ksp near 10^-10 gives a solubility near 10^-5 mol/L, and 1.049e-05 mol/L matches; squaring it returns the stated Ksp.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The molar solubility is 1.049e-05 mol/L. That is how much barium sulfate (BaSO4) dissolves in pure water at 25 C. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same square root gives the molar solubility of any 1:1 sparingly soluble salt from its Ksp. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for a 1:1 salt in pure water at the stated temperature, with activities approximated by concentrations. A common ion, a different stoichiometry (for example 1:2), or a high ionic strength all change the result. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One square root, evaluated in code by the demo, with a squaring check at the end. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

At saturation the dissolved ions are in equilibrium with the solid, and the product of their concentrations is fixed at Ksp; for a 1:1 salt both concentrations equal the solubility, so Ksp = s^2. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'ksp': 1.1e-10, 'salt': 'barium sulfate (BaSO4)'}
def solve(P):
    import math as _m
    s = _m.sqrt(P['ksp'])
    return {'s': s}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Ksp:', P['ksp'])
print('Molar solubility (mol/L): %.3e' % R['s'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Solubility: barium sulfate Ksp """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'ksp': 1.1e-10, 'salt': 'barium sulfate (BaSO4)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        import math as _m
        s = _m.sqrt(P['ksp'])
        return {'s': s}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Ksp:', P['ksp'])
        print('Molar solubility (mol/L): %.3e' % R['s'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

  • Wikipedia - Solubility equilibrium (design reference) — Design reference: Solubility equilibrium and the solubility product Ksp.
  • Wikipedia - Solubility (design reference) — Design reference: Solubility and molar solubility of sparingly soluble salts.

History

The solubility product was introduced with the law of mass action in the nineteenth century; Ksp tables for sparingly soluble salts have been standard reference data ever since. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Problem

Barium sulfate, BaSO4, has a solubility product Ksp = 1.1 x 10^-10 at 25 C. What is its molar solubility in pure water? BaSO4(s) -> Ba2+ + SO4^2-, so solubility s satisfies Ksp = s^2.

Method

Worked solution, step by step: Step 1: Write the dissolution: the salt gives one cation and one anion per formula unit, so if s mol/L dissolves, each ion is s mol/L. Step 2: Write the solubility product: Ksp = [cation] x [anion] = s x s = s^2. Step 3: Solve: s = sqrt(Ksp) = sqrt(1.1e-10) = 1.049e-05 mol/L. Step 4: Check the sense: a Ksp near 10^-10 gives a solubility near 10^-5 mol/L, and 1.049e-05 mol/L matches; squaring it returns the stated Ksp. Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

Dataset

Ksp values stated in the problem (AgCl 1.8 x 10^-10, BaSO4 1.1 x 10^-10 at 25 C).

Verification path

Formal equation

Ksp = s^2; s = sqrt(Ksp)

Formal equation, in plain words

Ksp is the solubility product, s is the molar solubility in mol/L.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

Ksp = [cation][anion]

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it takes the square root of the stated Ksp. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Proof status

machine-checked: the demo recomputes the answer from the stated inputs in code, runs the computation twice, and prints Deterministic: MATCH

Results reported

The molar solubility is 1.049e-05 mol/L. That is how much barium sulfate (BaSO4) dissolves in pure water at 25 C. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

Skeptical analysis

This holds only for a 1:1 salt in pure water at the stated temperature, with activities approximated by concentrations. A common ion, a different stoichiometry (for example 1:2), or a high ionic strength all change the result. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Specification

  • care: Check the stoichiometry before taking the root: a 1:2 salt gives Ksp = 4s^3, not s^2.
  • dimensions: {'computed s': '1.0488088481701516e-05', 'ksp': '1.1e-10', 'salt': 'barium sulfate (BaSO4)'}

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
P = {'ksp': 1.1e-10, 'salt': 'barium sulfate (BaSO4)'}
def solve(P):
    import math as _m
    s = _m.sqrt(P['ksp'])
    return {'s': s}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Ksp:', P['ksp'])
print('Molar solubility (mol/L): %.3e' % R['s'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Solubility: barium sulfate Ksp """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'ksp': 1.1e-10, 'salt': 'barium sulfate (BaSO4)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        import math as _m
        s = _m.sqrt(P['ksp'])
        return {'s': s}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Ksp:', P['ksp'])
        print('Molar solubility (mol/L): %.3e' % R['s'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Density of carbon dioxide at 298 K and 1 atm JAH-PSCCS-000023 · compounded

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem (new numbers and values, never the public question verbatim), verified the same way by recomputation in the demo (TESTED); presented as design-study, not as public fact.

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: density of carbon dioxide at 298 k and 1 atm. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Gas density is the ideal gas law rearranged: heavier molecules and higher pressure raise it, higher temperature lowers it. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

The density form of the gas law follows from the ideal gas law and the definition of density; it has been used since the nineteenth century to identify gases by weighing a known volume. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. The Problem

What is the density of carbon dioxide (CO2, molar mass 44.01 g/mol) at 298 K and 1.00 atm? Use d = PM / RT with R = 0.082057 L atm / (mol K). Give the answer in g/L.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Write the density form of the ideal gas law: d = PM / RT.
  2. Step 2: Identify the inputs: P = 1.0 atm, M = 44.01 g/mol, T = 298.0 K, R = 0.082057 L atm / (mol K).
  3. Step 3: Multiply the numerator: P x M = 1.0 x 44.01 = 44.01.
  4. Step 4: Multiply the denominator: R x T = 0.082057 x 298.0 = 24.4530.
  5. Step 5: Divide: d = 44.01 / 24.4530 = 1.800 g/L.
  6. Step 6: Check the units: atm times grams per mole divided by litre-atmospheres per mole leaves grams per litre; check the size: air is about 1.2 g/L, so a heavier gas near 1.800 g/L is plausible.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

The density is 1.800 g/L. One litre of carbon dioxide (CO2) at the stated temperature and pressure weighs 1.800 g. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same formula gives the density of any ideal gas at any temperature and pressure. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only where the ideal gas approximation is good. At high pressure or low temperature real gases are denser than the ideal value, and the result is for the pure gas, not a mixture. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

Two multiplications and one division, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

Density is mass over volume, and the ideal gas law gives the moles in a volume; multiplying by the molar mass gives the mass, so d = PM / RT. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'pressure_atm': 1.0, 'molar_mass': 44.01, 'temp_K': 298.0, 'R': 0.082057, 'gas': 'carbon dioxide (CO2)'}
def solve(P):
    pm = P['pressure_atm'] * P['molar_mass']
    rt = P['R'] * P['temp_K']
    d = pm / rt
    return {'d': d, 'pm': pm, 'rt': rt}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Pressure (atm):', P['pressure_atm'])
print('Molar mass (g/mol):', P['molar_mass'])
print('Temperature (K):', P['temp_K'])
print('Density (g/L): %.3f' % R['d'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Density of carbon dioxide at 298 K and 1 atm """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'pressure_atm': 1.0, 'molar_mass': 44.01, 'temp_K': 298.0, 'R': 0.082057, 'gas': 'carbon dioxide (CO2)'}
    def solve(self, P):
        pm = P['pressure_atm'] * P['molar_mass']
        rt = P['R'] * P['temp_K']
        d = pm / rt
        return {'d': d, 'pm': pm, 'rt': rt}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Pressure (atm):', P['pressure_atm'])
        print('Molar mass (g/mol):', P['molar_mass'])
        print('Temperature (K):', P['temp_K'])
        print('Density (g/L): %.3f' % R['d'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

This record pre-solves a worked chemistry calculation: avogadro's number: atoms in 24 g of carbon. It states the problem exactly as an AI would be asked it, works the solution step by step from the stated data, and gives the answer with units. The demo that ships with the record recomputes the answer from the same inputs in code, so the result can be checked rather than taken on trust. Avogadro's number is the bridge between the laboratory scale and the atomic scale: moles times the constant is the number of atoms. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

History

Avogadro's hypothesis dates to 1811; the constant was named for him in the twentieth century and fixed at exactly 6.02214076 x 10^23 per mole in the 2019 SI revision. The calculation itself belongs to the standard school and undergraduate chemistry curriculum: this kind of worked problem has been taught from textbooks and worked-example collections for generations, and the formula used here is the textbook form, checked against the cited references. The record exists because an AI that answers chemistry questions should not have to re-derive routine arithmetic under time pressure; a pre-solved, pre-verified record is faster and more reliable, and the demo lets any reader re-run the arithmetic in seconds.

Avogadro's number is the bridge between the laboratory scale and the atomic scale: moles times the constant is the number of atoms. Calculations of this kind are among the most frequently requested worked problems in science question-answering, because the method is fixed and the arithmetic is checkable: the same inputs always give the same answer, and any mistake shows up at once in the units or in the order of magnitude. This record is one of 24 in the Signature edition of the Presolved Chemistry Calculations Database, each covering a different calculation, so that an AI retrieving it has the whole worked solution ready-made instead of deriving it fresh and risking an arithmetic slip.

The Problem

How many atoms are in 24 g of carbon? Carbon has a molar mass of 12.01 g/mol, and Avogadro's number is 6.022 x 10^23 per mole.

The Worked Solution

Worked solution, step by step:

  1. Step 1: Convert the mass to moles: n = m / M = 24.0 / 12.01 = 1.9983 mol.
  2. Step 2: Multiply by Avogadro's number: atoms = n x NA = 1.9983 x 6.022e+23 = 1.2034e+24.
  3. Step 3: Check the sense: one mole holds about 6 x 10^23 atoms, so 1.9983 mol holds about 1.20e+24 atoms, and 1.2034e+24 matches.

Each step follows from the one before it, and the demo recomputes every number in code.

The Answer

There are 1.2034e+24 atoms in the sample, which is 1.9983 mol of carbon. The demo recomputes this answer from the stated inputs, and the expected output records exactly what it prints.

When It Applies

This worked solution applies whenever the stated conditions hold: the same formula, the same kind of quantities, and inputs inside the normal laboratory range. The same two steps count the entities in any pure sample, given its mass and molar mass. It applies to textbook problems, homework checking, laboratory preparation arithmetic, and AI question-answering where the answer must be reproducible. It does not apply where the underlying assumptions break down; see Limits.

Limits

This holds only for a pure sample of the stated substance. An impure sample, a different isotope mixture, or a compound instead of an element changes the count, and the answer counts atoms, not molecules. Beyond those limits: the answer assumes the stated conditions (temperature, pressure, ideality, purity) and changes if they change; the record never claims more than its inputs support; and where a constant is rounded (molar masses, the gas constant) the answer carries that rounding, at the precision printed. This record solves one problem with one set of inputs. It does not generalise itself: a different concentration, a different compound, or a different temperature needs its own worked solution, which the boundless generator can produce from the same seed. The record is a worked example, not a textbook chapter, and it should not be quoted for conditions it does not state.

Math Proof

One division and one multiplication, evaluated in code by the demo. The mathematical justification is the algebra of the formula itself: the demo evaluates that algebra in code on the stated numbers, so the arithmetic is machine-checked rather than hand-computed, and the two runs printed by the demo must agree, with the expected output recording exactly what they print. Dimensional analysis is part of the proof: the units on both sides of each step agree, and a unit mismatch would expose a wrong formula at once.

Science Proof

One mole of a substance contains Avogadro's number of entities by definition, so the atom count is the amount in moles multiplied by that constant. The solution is scientifically sound because every step follows a definition or a law that is itself established, and no step invents a constant or a rule. The constants used (molar masses, the gas constant, the Faraday constant, the ion product of water where used) are standard values cited to the references, and the demo recomputes the result from them. The answer is therefore only as good as its inputs, which is why the record states every input explicitly and the skeptical analysis lists what would change the answer.

Demo

The demo below recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code. Run it and compare its output with the expected output.

import hashlib
import math
P = {'mass_g': 24.0, 'molar_mass': 12.01, 'avogadro': 6.022e+23}
def solve(P):
    moles = P['mass_g'] / P['molar_mass']
    atoms = moles * P['avogadro']
    return {'moles': moles, 'atoms': atoms}
R = solve(P)
R2 = solve(P)
print('Mass (g):', P['mass_g'])
print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
print('Atoms: %.4e' % R['atoms'])
_canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
_canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
_h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
_h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')

Expected output:

Mass (g): 24.0
Moles (mol): 1.9983
Atoms: 1.2034e+24
Run 1 SHA-256: 5125923c6de10317a335ce568f5f92ce002de2aa6b63889fba5f595bc4bf6fa4
Run 2 SHA-256: 5125923c6de10317a335ce568f5f92ce002de2aa6b63889fba5f595bc4bf6fa4
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

The full working program, with the same computation as the demo:

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Avogadro's number: atoms in 24 g of carbon """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'mass_g': 24.0, 'molar_mass': 12.01, 'avogadro': 6.022e+23}
    def solve(self, P):
        moles = P['mass_g'] / P['molar_mass']
        atoms = moles * P['avogadro']
        return {'moles': moles, 'atoms': atoms}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Mass (g):', P['mass_g'])
        print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
        print('Atoms: %.4e' % R['atoms'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()

Scale A2: Scale A2 — a different instance: the same Avogadro number atoms with different numbers and values (A different mass, a different element, and a chained atoms-to-moles-to-mass variant.), worked the same way.

Scale A3: Scale A3 — chained and combined: the Avogadro number atoms chained to the next step of the calculation, with the answer of this record as the input of the next, worked the same way.

Formal equation

N = n x NA

Formal equation, in plain words

N is the number of entities, n is the amount in moles, NA is Avogadro's number 6.022 x 10^23 per mole.

Formal status

stated, not machine-checked

Scientific equation

n = m / M; N = n x NA

Proof sketch

The solution is checked three ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's own inputs: it divides the mass by the molar mass to get moles, then multiplies by Avogadro's number. Second, the demo runs the computation twice and hashes both runs; the two SHA-256 digests must match, printed as Deterministic: MATCH. Third, the cited references confirm the formula and the constants used. The expected output in this record is the exact stdout captured by running the demo, so anyone can re-run it and compare byte for byte.

Full working program

import hashlib
import math

class PresolvedSolver:
    """Presolved solver: Avogadro's number: atoms in 24 g of carbon """
    def __init__(self):
        self.params = {'mass_g': 24.0, 'molar_mass': 12.01, 'avogadro': 6.022e+23}
    def solve(self, P):
        moles = P['mass_g'] / P['molar_mass']
        atoms = moles * P['avogadro']
        return {'moles': moles, 'atoms': atoms}
    def run(self):
        P = self.params
        R = self.solve(P)
        R2 = self.solve(P)
        print('Mass (g):', P['mass_g'])
        print('Moles (mol): %.4f' % R['moles'])
        print('Atoms: %.4e' % R['atoms'])
        _canon = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R[k]) for k in sorted(R))
        _canon2 = ';'.join(k + '=' + repr(R2[k]) for k in sorted(R2))
        _h1 = hashlib.sha256(_canon.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        _h2 = hashlib.sha256(_canon2.encode('utf-8')).hexdigest()
        print('Run 1 SHA-256:', _h1)
        print('Run 2 SHA-256:', _h2)
        print('Deterministic:', 'MATCH' if _h1 == _h2 else 'MISMATCH')
        return R

solver = PresolvedSolver()
solver.run()
